Learn English/Parts of speech/04-adjectives/catalog

More adjectives: B–C#

Adjectives B–C beyond the grammar groups — the everyday adjectives that a triage of WordNet’s adjective lemmas showed a learner needs.

← The grammar of adjectives · All groups

Most English adjectives behave: they go before a noun or after be, and they compare with -er or more. These are those — but each still says how it compares, where it can stand, and what it takes. WordNet lists about twenty thousand adjectives; most of the rest are technical, archaic or British-only, and a triage pass left them out on purpose.

baby-faced#

Pronunciation
/ˈbeɪbiˌfeɪst/ · BAY-bee-fayst
Русский
с детским лицом
Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — baby + face + -ed

Having a young-looking, round face like a child's.

  1. The baby-faced quarterback is actually thirty.
  2. He looks baby-faced, but he's the oldest guy on the team.
  3. A baby-faced actor played the young president.

Babylonian#

Pronunciation
/ˌbæbəˈloʊniən/ · bab-uh-LOH-nee-uhn
Русский
вавилонский
Type
origin
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ian from Babylon

From or relating to ancient Babylon.

  1. The Babylonian king Hammurabi wrote one of the first law codes.
  2. Babylonian astronomers tracked the movements of the planets.
  3. Experts believe the clay tablet is Babylonian.

back#

Pronunciation
/bæk/ · BAK
Русский
задний; прошлый; просроченный
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only — the back door; after a verb use at the back or in the back
Comparison
not gradable
Opposite
front
Contrast
The adjective only goes before a noun: ✗ The door is back → The door is in the back. Back is much more often an adverb (come back), a noun (my back hurts) or a verb (back up).

Back has 3 distinct senses.

1. At or near the rear of something. (задний)

  1. Let's use the back door.
  2. The kids sat in the back seat.
  3. We had coffee on the back porch.

2. From an earlier time; owed or published before now. (прошлый, просроченный)

  1. He owes three years of back taxes.
  2. The library keeps back issues of the magazine.

3. Away from main roads and busy places. (глухой, окольный)

  1. We took the back roads to avoid traffic.
  2. They live on a quiet back street.

back-channel#

Pronunciation
/ˈbækˌtʃænəl/ · BAK-chan-uhl
Русский
неофициальный; закулисный
Type
compound
Position
attributive only — back-channel talks
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — back + channel

Secret and unofficial, outside the normal channels.

  1. The two governments held back-channel talks for months.
  2. She used her back-channel contacts to get the information.
  3. The deal came out of back-channel negotiations.

back-to-back#

Pronunciation
/ˌbæktəˈbæk/ · bak-tuh-BAK
Русский
идущие подряд
Type
compound
Position
attributive only — back-to-back wins
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — back + to + back
Contrast
As an adverb, written without hyphens, it means with backs touching (stand back to back) or one after another (two games back to back).

Happening one right after the other.

  1. The Warriors won back-to-back championships.
  2. I have back-to-back meetings all afternoon.
  3. We had back-to-back snowstorms last week.

backbreaking#

Pronunciation
/ˈbækˌbreɪkɪŋ/ · BAK-bray-king
Русский
изнурительный
Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more backbreaking, most backbreaking
Form
compound — back + breaking

Very hard and physically tiring.

  1. Picking cotton was backbreaking work.
  2. The job was more backbreaking than I expected.
  3. They spent a backbreaking summer building the barn.

backhanded#

Pronunciation
/ˈbækˌhændɪd/ · BAK-han-did
Русский
сомнительный; двусмысленный; наотмашь
Type
quality
Position
attributive only — a backhanded compliment
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — back + hand + -ed

Backhanded has 2 distinct senses.

1. Seeming to praise but actually criticizing. (сомнительный, двусмысленный)

  1. 'You look great for your age' is a backhanded compliment.
  2. She's an expert at backhanded praise.

2. Done with the back of the hand facing forward. (наотмашь; (в теннисе) слева)

  1. He hit a backhanded shot down the line.
  2. She gave him a backhanded slap.

backstage#

Pronunciation
/ˈbækˌsteɪdʒ/ · BAK-stayj
Русский
закулисный
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only — a backstage pass
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
More common as an adverb, stressed on the second syllable: We went backstage after the show.

Behind the stage, or private and out of public view.

  1. I got a backstage pass for the concert.
  2. The documentary shows the backstage drama of a Broadway show.
  3. There was a lot of backstage arguing before the deal was signed.

backward#

Pronunciation
/ˈbækwərd/ · BAK-wurd
Русский
обратный; отсталый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more backward, most backward
Opposite
forward
Contrast
In American English the adjective is backward; backwards is only an adverb (walk backwards), and backward can be the adverb too. Calling people or places backward sounds insulting.

Backward has 2 distinct senses.

1. Directed toward the back or the starting point. (обратный, направленный назад)

  1. She took a backward step.
  2. He left without a backward glance.

2. Less developed or modern than others. (отсталый)

  1. The region was once one of the most backward in the state.
  2. Some people still hold backward ideas about women.

bacterial#

Pronunciation
/bækˈtɪriəl/ · bak-TEER-ee-uhl
Русский
бактериальный
Type
classifying
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-al from bacteria

Caused by or relating to bacteria.

  1. Antibiotics only work on bacterial infections.
  2. Strep throat is bacterial, not viral.
  3. The lab tests water for bacterial growth.

bad-mannered#

Pronunciation
/ˌbædˈmænərd/ · bad-MAN-urd
Русский
невоспитанный
Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more bad-mannered, most bad-mannered
Form
compound — bad + manner + -ed
Opposite
well-mannered

Behaving rudely; not polite.

  1. The bad-mannered guests left without saying goodbye.
  2. It's bad-mannered to check your phone at dinner.
  3. He was the most bad-mannered kid in the class.

bad-tempered#

Pronunciation
/ˌbædˈtɛmpərd/ · bad-TEM-purd
Русский
раздражительный; сердитый
Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more bad-tempered, most bad-tempered
Form
compound — bad + temper + -ed
Opposite
good-natured

Easily annoyed or angry.

  1. Our neighbor is a bad-tempered old man.
  2. She gets bad-tempered when she's hungry.
  3. The meeting got more bad-tempered as it went on.

baffled#

Pronunciation
/ˈbæfəld/ · BAF-uhld
Русский
озадаченный; сбитый с толку
Type
participial; also emotion
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more baffled, completely baffled
Takes
by + noun
Form
-ed — how someone feels
Contrast
baffled is the person who does not understand; baffling is the thing that causes it: I was baffled by the baffling instructions.

Completely unable to understand something.

  1. Doctors were baffled by her symptoms.
  2. He gave me a baffled look.
  3. I'm even more baffled now than before.

baffling#

Pronunciation
/ˈbæflɪŋ/ · BAF-ling
Русский
непонятный; загадочный
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more baffling, most baffling
Form
-ing — what causes the feeling
Contrast
baffling is the thing; baffled is the person who does not understand it.

Impossible to understand or explain.

  1. The instructions were completely baffling.
  2. Police called it a baffling case.
  3. The most baffling part is that nobody saw anything.

baggy#

Pronunciation
/ˈbæɡi/ · BAG-ee
Русский
мешковатый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — baggier, baggiest
Form
-y from bag
Opposite
tight

Hanging loosely, because it is too big.

  1. He wore baggy jeans and a hoodie.
  2. My pants got baggier after I lost weight.
  3. The sweater was a little baggy on him.

baked#

/beɪkt/ · BAYKT

печёный; запечённый

-ed from bake

Cooked in an oven with dry heat.

  1. I'll have a baked potato with sour cream.
  2. Is the chicken fried or baked?
  3. The church sells baked goods every Sunday.

baking hot#

Pronunciation
/ˌbeɪkɪŋ ˈhɑːt/ · bay-king HAHT
Русский
раскалённый; очень жаркий
Type
compound
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
baking makes hot stronger
Contrast
An extreme adjective, so not very baking hot. Americans also just say baking: It's baking out there!

Extremely hot.

  1. It was baking hot inside the car.
  2. We spent a baking hot afternoon at the ballpark.
  3. Don't leave the dog outside; it's baking hot.

balanced#

Pronunciation
/ˈbælənst/ · BAL-uhnst
Русский
сбалансированный; взвешенный; уравновешенный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more balanced, most balanced
Opposite
unbalanced

Balanced has 3 distinct senses.

1. Having the right amounts of different things. (сбалансированный)

  1. Try to eat a balanced diet.
  2. The team has a more balanced offense this year.

2. Fair, giving all sides equal attention. (взвешенный, объективный)

  1. The article gives a balanced view of the issue.
  2. Viewers want balanced news coverage.

3. Calm and sensible. (уравновешенный)

  1. She's a very balanced person.
  2. He seems well-balanced despite everything he's been through.

bald#

Pronunciation
/bɔːld/ · BAWLD
Русский
лысый; стёртый; неприкрытый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — balder, baldest

Bald has 3 distinct senses.

1. Having little or no hair on the head. (лысый)

  1. My dad started going bald in his thirties.
  2. He's balder than his older brother.
  3. A bald man in a suit opened the door.

2. Worn smooth or without the usual covering. (стёртый, голый)

  1. You need new tires; these are bald.
  2. The lawn has bald spots after the drought.

3. Plain and direct, with nothing added to soften it. (голый, неприкрытый)

  1. The report was a bald statement of the facts.
  2. That's a bald lie.

bald-faced#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɔːldˌfeɪst/ · BAWLD-fayst
Русский
наглый; неприкрытый
Type
compound
Position
attributive only — a bald-faced lie
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — bald + face + -ed
Contrast
Almost always a bald-faced lie. Barefaced means the same.

Open and shameless, with no attempt to hide it.

  1. That's a bald-faced lie.
  2. It was a bald-faced attempt to buy votes.
  3. He told a bald-faced lie to the judge.

balding#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɔːldɪŋ/ · BAWL-ding
Русский
лысеющий
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ing from bald

Starting to lose the hair on the head.

  1. A balding man in his fifties sat next to me.
  2. He's balding and a little overweight.
  3. My uncle was already balding at twenty-five.

ballistic#

Pronunciation
/bəˈlɪstɪk/ · buh-LIS-tik
Русский
баллистический; взбешённый
Type
classifying; also emotion
Position
both — before a noun in the missile sense; after go in the angry sense
Comparison
not gradable

Ballistic has 2 distinct senses.

1. Relating to missiles or bullets and how they fly. (баллистический)

  1. The country tested a ballistic missile.
  2. Police sent the bullets for ballistic testing.

2. Extremely angry, in go ballistic (informal). (взбешённый)

  1. My mom went ballistic when she saw the phone bill.
  2. The coach went ballistic at the referee.

balmy#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɑːmi/ · BAH-mee
Русский
тёплый; мягкий (о погоде)
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — balmier, balmiest
Contrast
The l is silent: BAH-mee.

Pleasantly warm, of weather or air.

  1. It was a balmy evening in Miami.
  2. The weather gets balmier as you drive south.
  3. We had the balmiest February on record.

Made with dark, sweet Italian vinegar.

  1. Drizzle the salad with balsamic vinegar.
  2. The tomatoes come with a balsamic glaze.
  3. She served the chicken with a balsamic reduction.

Wrapped in a bandage.

bang-up#

Pronunciation
/ˈbæŋˌʌp/ · BANG-uhp
Русский
отличный
Type
evaluative
Position

Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
American informal. Not the same as banged-up, which means damaged or bruised.

Excellent (informal).

  1. You did a bang-up job on the kitchen.
  2. The team had a bang-up season.
  3. They threw a bang-up party for his retirement.

bankable#

Pronunciation
/ˈbæŋkəbəl/ · BANG-kuh-buhl
Русский
прибыльный; кассовый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison

-able from bank

Sure to make money.

  1. She's one of the most bankable stars in Hollywood.
  2. Superhero movies are still bankable.
  3. The investors didn't think the idea was bankable.

bankrupt#

Pronunciation
/ˈbæŋkrʌpt/ · BANGK-ruhpt
Русский
обанкротившийся; лишённый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Takes
of + noun, in the sense 'completely lacking'
Contrast
Say go bankrupt. Bankrupt is also a verb (The lawsuit bankrupted them) and a noun (a bankrupt).

Bankrupt has 2 distinct senses.

1. Officially unable to pay one's debts. (обанкротившийся)

  1. The company went bankrupt in 2009.
  2. A bankrupt airline left thousands of passengers stuck.

2. Completely without a good quality. (лишённый (чего-то), несостоятельный)

  1. Critics called the government morally bankrupt.
  2. The campaign is bankrupt of new ideas.

Pronunciation
/bænd/ · BAND
Русский
запрещённый
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Takes
from + noun or -ing
Form
-ed from ban

Officially not allowed.

  1. The athlete tested positive for a banned substance.
  2. Fireworks are banned in this county.
  3. He's banned from the gym for a month.

banner#

Pronunciation
/ˈbænər/ · BAN-ur
Русский
рекордный; выдающийся
Type
evaluative
Position
attributive only — a banner year
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
Only before a noun, mostly a banner year or a banner season. The noun banner is a flag or a long sign.

Unusually good or successful.

  1. It was a banner year for the company.
  2. Farmers had a banner harvest.
  3. The Braves are coming off a banner season.

Having gone through the Christian ceremony of baptism.

  1. She's a baptized Catholic.
  2. Only baptized members can take communion.
  3. He was raised in the church but was never baptized.

Cooked slowly over a fire or on a grill, often with a sauce.

barbed#

Pronunciation
/bɑːrbd/ · BARBD
Русский
колючий; язвительный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more barbed, most barbed
Form
-ed from barb 'a sharp point'

Barbed has 2 distinct senses.

1. Having sharp points that stick out backward. (колючий, с шипами)

  1. The farm is surrounded by a barbed wire fence.
  2. The hook is barbed so the fish can't get off.

2. Meant to hurt someone's feelings. (язвительный, колкий)

  1. She made a barbed comment about his weight.
  2. His jokes got more barbed as the night went on.

bare#

Pronunciation
/bɛr/ · BAIR
Русский
голый; пустой; минимальный
Type
quality
Position
both — before a noun only in the sense 'the smallest amount'
Comparison
-er/-est — barer, barest
Contrast
Sounds exactly like bear. For 'naked' about a whole person Americans usually say naked; bare is for parts of the body and surfaces.

Bare has 3 distinct senses.

1. Not covered by clothes or anything else. (голый, босой, непокрытый)

  1. Don't walk on the hot sand in bare feet.
  2. The trees were bare by November.
  3. He slept on the bare floor.

2. Empty; without the usual things. (пустой)

  1. Store shelves were bare before the hurricane.
  2. The apartment still had bare walls.

3. Only the most basic; the smallest amount needed. (самый необходимый, минимальный)

  1. We packed only the bare necessities.
  2. He did the bare minimum to pass.
  3. There wasn't even the barest hint of a smile.

bare-knuckle#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɛrˌnʌkəl/ · BAIR-nuhk-uhl
Русский
кулачный; беспощадный
Type
compound
Position
attributive only — a bare-knuckle fight
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — bare + knuckle

Bare-knuckle has 2 distinct senses.

1. Fought with fists and no boxing gloves. (кулачный, без перчаток)

  1. Bare-knuckle boxing is legal in some states.
  2. The two men got into a bare-knuckle fight outside the bar.

2. Aggressive and without any rules or restraint. (беспощадный, жёсткий)

  1. It was a bare-knuckle political campaign.
  2. The firm is known for bare-knuckle business tactics.

bareback#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɛrˌbæk/ · BAIR-bak
Русский
без седла
Type
compound
Position
attributive only — a bareback rider
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — bare + back
Contrast
More often an adverb: She rode bareback.

Riding a horse without a saddle.

  1. Bareback riding is one of the toughest rodeo events.
  2. The circus had a bareback rider in a sparkly costume.
  3. The ranch offers bareback lessons for experienced riders.

barefoot#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɛrfʊt/ · BAIR-fuut
Русский
босой
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — bare + foot
Contrast
Also an adverb (walk barefoot), with the same form. Barefooted exists but is less common.

Not wearing shoes or socks.

  1. She was barefoot when she answered the door.
  2. Two barefoot kids were playing in the yard.
  3. Is it OK to go barefoot in here?

barometric#

Pronunciation
/ˌbærəˈmɛtrɪk/ · bair-uh-MET-rik
Русский
барометрический; атмосферный
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only — barometric pressure
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ic from barometer

Relating to air pressure or a barometer.

  1. Barometric pressure drops before a storm.
  2. Changes in barometric pressure give me headaches.
  3. The barometric reading was unusually low.

baroque#

Pronunciation
/bəˈroʊk/ · buh-ROHK
Русский
барочный; вычурный
Type
classifying; also quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more baroque, most baroque
Contrast
For the historical style it is often written with a capital: Baroque music.

Baroque has 2 distinct senses.

1. In the style of European art, buildings and music of about 1600 to 1750. (барочный, в стиле барокко)

  1. We went to a concert of baroque music.
  2. The church has a painted baroque ceiling.

2. Too complicated or full of decoration. (вычурный, запутанный)

  1. The tax rules are baroque.
  2. His writing is even more baroque than his speeches.

barred#

Pronunciation
/bɑːrd/ · BARD
Русский
зарешёченный; не допущенный
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Takes
from + noun or -ing, in the sense 'not allowed'
Form
-ed from bar

Barred has 2 distinct senses.

1. Having bars across it. (зарешёченный, запертый на засов)

  1. The house has barred windows.
  2. The door was barred from the inside.

2. Not allowed to do something or go somewhere. (не допущенный, лишённый права)

  1. He is barred from entering the country.
  2. Reporters were barred from the meeting.

barren#

Pronunciation
/ˈbærən/ · BAIR-uhn
Русский
бесплодный; пустынный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more barren, most barren
Takes
of + noun, in the sense 'without'
Opposite
fertile
Contrast
Barren for a woman who cannot have children is old-fashioned and now sounds offensive.

Barren has 2 distinct senses.

1. Too dry or poor for plants to grow. (бесплодный, голый, пустынный)

  1. The land around the town is flat and barren.
  2. They drove through miles of barren desert.
  3. The hills looked even more barren in winter.

2. Producing no results or useful ideas. (бесплодный, безрезультатный)

  1. It was a barren period in her career.
  2. The talks were barren of progress.

base#

Pronunciation
/beɪs/ · BAYS
Русский
низменный; неблагородный
Type
quality
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
-er/-est — baser, basest
Contrast
Formal. Base is far more often a noun (a military base) or a verb (based on).

Base has 2 distinct senses.

1. Morally low; without honor. (низменный, подлый)

  1. The ad appeals to people's basest instincts.
  2. Greed is one of the baser human emotions.
  3. He was driven by base motives.

2. Of a metal: common and not valuable. (неблагородный (о металле))

  1. Alchemists tried to turn base metals into gold.
  2. The ring is a base metal with gold plating.

Pronunciation

Contrast

Based has 2 distinct senses.

  1. The company is based in Seattle.

2. Using something as the starting point or source. (основанный)

  1. The movie is based on a true story.
  2. Her decision was based on the facts.

basic#

Pronunciation
/ˈbeɪsɪk/ · BAY-sik
Русский

Type

Position

Comparison

Form

Opposite

Contrast
Russian основной often means main (the main idea), not basic. Basic is 'the simplest, necessary level'.

Basic has 3 distinct senses.

1. Forming the main or most necessary part. (основной, базовый)

  1. Food and shelter are basic human needs.
  2. First learn the basic rules of the game.

2. Simple, with nothing extra. (простой, элементарный)

  1. The hotel room was pretty basic.
  2. I only know some basic Spanish.
  3. This is the most basic model they sell.

3. Ordinary and unoriginal, following popular trends (slang). (банальный, заурядный)

  1. Pumpkin spice lattes? That's so basic.
  2. She thinks his taste in music is kind of basic.

Pronunciation

Form

Contrast
Off the beaten path is the usual American form; British English says off the beaten track.

Beaten has 3 distinct senses.

1. Defeated, and feeling that you have lost. (побеждённый, сломленный)

  1. The beaten team walked slowly off the field.
  2. After the third rejection he felt beaten.

2. (Of a path) worn flat by many people walking on it. (проторённый)

  1. We followed a beaten path through the woods.
  2. The best restaurants are off the beaten path.

3. (Of eggs or cream) mixed quickly until smooth. (взбитый)

  1. Add two beaten eggs to the flour.
  2. Brush the dough with beaten egg before baking.

Pronunciation
/bɪˈhaɪnd/ · bih-HYND

Behind has 2 distinct senses.

  1. The Cubs were behind by three runs in the seventh.
  2. She's still behind in the polls, but the gap is closing.

bemused#

Comparison

Contrast
A trap: bemused traditionally means 'puzzled', not 'amused'. Many Americans now use it for 'mildly amused', so context decides; careful writers keep it for 'puzzled'.

Bemused has 2 distinct senses.

2. Mildly amused, often because something seems odd. (снисходительно-насмешливый)

  1. He watched the kids' argument with a bemused smile.
  2. She read the angry comments with bemused detachment.

benign#

Pronunciation

both
Comparison

Opposite

Contrast
The g is silent: it rhymes with nine.

Benign has 3 distinct senses.

  2. The doctor removed a small benign growth.

2. Not harmful or dangerous. (безвредный, безобидный)

  1. Most of these apps are fairly benign.

3. Kind, gentle or mild. (добрый, мягкий)

  1. He had a benign smile.
  2. The weather was benign all week.

bent#

Pronunciation
/bɛnt/ · BENT
Русский

Type

Position

Comparison

Form
past participle of bend
Opposite

Contrast
Bent on something means 'determined to do it' — see bent on. British slang bent 'dishonest' is not used in American English.

Bent has 3 distinct senses.

1. No longer straight; curved or folded. (согнутый, погнутый)

  1. The nail was bent, so I pulled it out.
  2. Keep your knees bent when you lift the box.
  3. The front wheel of my bike is bent.

2. (Of a person) bending forward. (сгорбленный, согнувшийся)

  1. An old man, bent with age, shuffled past.
  2. She sat bent over her desk all afternoon.

3. (bent out of shape, informal) Upset or angry. (расстроенный, взвинченный)

  1. Don't get all bent out of shape about it.
  2. He got bent out of shape over a parking ticket.

/baɪˈlɪŋɡwəl/ · by-LING-gwuhl
Русский
двуязычный
Type
classifying
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
bi- (two) + Latin lingua (tongue)

Speaking two languages well, or using two languages.

  1. Their kids are bilingual in English and Spanish.
  2. The school offers bilingual classes.
  3. Road signs in the area are bilingual.

billowing#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɪloʊɪŋ/ · BIL-oh-ing
Русский
клубящийся, вздымающийся
Type
participial
Position
attributive only — after a verb it is the verb billow: smoke was billowing
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ing from billow

Rising or spreading out in large waves or clouds.

  1. Billowing smoke rose from the warehouse.
  2. She wore a white dress with billowing sleeves.
  3. We watched the billowing storm clouds roll in.

bimonthly#

Pronunciation
/baɪˈmʌnθli/ · by-MUNTH-lee
Русский
выходящий раз в два месяца; дважды в месяц
Type
classifying
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
bi- + monthly
Contrast
Ambiguous: usually 'every two months', but sometimes 'twice a month'. Every other month or twice a month (semimonthly) is clearer. Also an adverb: The magazine comes out bimonthly.

Bimonthly has 2 distinct senses.

1. Happening every two months. (выходящий раз в два месяца)

  1. The newsletter is bimonthly.
  2. We get a bimonthly water bill.

2. Happening twice a month. (проходящий дважды в месяц)

  1. The club holds bimonthly meetings on the first and third Tuesdays.
  2. The bimonthly farmers market runs on the 1st and the 15th.

binary#

Pronunciation
/ˈbaɪnəri/ · BY-nuh-ree
Русский
двоичный; бинарный
Type
classifying
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable

Binary has 2 distinct senses.

1. Using only the digits 0 and 1. (двоичный)

  1. Computers store data in binary code.
  2. Write the number 5 in binary form.

2. Having only two parts or two options. (бинарный, двойственный)

  1. Life isn't a binary choice between success and failure.
  2. Many people reject a binary view of gender.

binding#

Pronunciation
/ˈbaɪndɪŋ/ · BYN-ding
Русский

Type

Position

Comparison
not gradable
Takes
on or upon + noun
Preposition patterns
binding on · binding upon
Form
-ing from bind (to tie; to oblige)
Contrast
The binding (noun) is the cover and spine of a book, or the part of a ski that holds the boot.

(Of an agreement or decision) that must be obeyed by law.

  1. The contract is legally binding.
  2. The decision is binding on both parties.
  3. They signed a binding agreement.

biological#

Pronunciation
/ˌbaɪəˈlɑːdʒɪkəl/ · by-uh-LAH-jih-kuhl
Русский
биологический; родной (по крови); бактериологический
Type
classifying
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-al from biology
Contrast
For parents, biological is the neutral word; real mother can hurt adoptive families. Russian родной is wider: родной язык is native language, not biological.

Biological has 3 distinct senses.

1. Relating to living things or to biology. (биологический)

  1. Sleep is a basic biological need.
  2. The causes of the disease are partly biological.

2. Related by birth, not by adoption or marriage. (родной, биологический (по крови))

  1. She found her biological mother at age thirty.
  2. He's not my biological dad, but he raised me.

3. (Of weapons) using germs to cause disease. (бактериологический, биологический)

  1. The treaty bans biological weapons.
  2. Officials trained for a possible biological attack.

Pronunciation

Type

bio- + medical

Relating to both biology and medicine.

  1. He studied biomedical engineering.
  2. The company makes biomedical devices like pacemakers.
  3. The grant funds biomedical research on cancer.

bionic#

Pronunciation
/baɪˈɑːnɪk/ · by-AH-nik
Русский
бионический
Type
classifying
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable

Bionic has 2 distinct senses.

1. Having or being electronic body parts. (бионический)

  1. The veteran walks on a bionic leg.
  2. Researchers are testing a bionic eye.

2. (Informal) Stronger or better than a normal person. (сверхчеловеческий)

  1. Our goalie has bionic reflexes.
  2. She must have bionic hearing — she heard us from upstairs.

Supported by or involving both main political parties.

bipolar#

Pronunciation
/baɪˈpoʊlər/ · by-POH-lur
Русский
биполярный
Type
classifying
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
bi- + polar
Contrast
Using bipolar casually for 'moody' (the weather is so bipolar) is common but offends many people.

Bipolar has 2 distinct senses.

1. Having bipolar disorder, a mental illness with extreme changes of mood. (страдающий биполярным расстройством)

  1. Her brother was diagnosed with bipolar disorder.
  2. He's bipolar and takes medication for it.

2. Having two opposite poles or two main powers. (биполярный, двухполюсный)

  1. The bipolar world of the Cold War ended in 1991.
  2. Scientists studied the star's bipolar jets of gas.

bitchy#

quality — offensive slang

Comparison
-er/-est — bitchier, bitchiest
Contrast
Rude slang, mostly about women; avoid it in polite or professional speech.

Mean and unkind in what you say about people.

  1. She made a bitchy remark about my hair.

biting#

Pronunciation
/ˈbaɪtɪŋ/ · BY-ting
Русский
пронизывающий; язвительный
Type
participial
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
more/most — more biting, most biting
Form
-ing from bite

Biting has 2 distinct senses.

1. (Of wind or cold) so cold that it hurts. (пронизывающий, резкий)

  1. We walked home in the biting wind.
  2. The cold that morning was biting.

2. (Of words) sharp and cruel. (язвительный, едкий)

  1. The critic wrote a biting review.
  2. Her humor is more biting than her sister's.

bitter#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɪtər/ · BIT-ur
Русский
горький; озлобленный; ожесточённый; лютый
Type
quality; also emotion
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est or more/most — bitterer or more bitter, bitterest
Takes
about or at + noun (feeling resentment)
Opposite
sweet (taste)
Contrast
Russian горький перец is hot pepper in English: bitter is the taste of coffee, dark chocolate or medicine, never spicy heat.

Bitter has 5 distinct senses.

1. Having a strong, sharp taste that is not sweet. (горький)

  1. This coffee is too bitter for me.
  2. The longer the tea steeps, the more bitter it gets.

2. Angry and upset because you think you were treated unfairly. (озлобленный, обиженный)

  1. He's still bitter about losing his job.
  2. She gave a short, bitter laugh.

3. Making you very unhappy; hard to accept. (горький, тяжёлый)

  1. The loss was a bitter disappointment.
  2. She learned from bitter experience not to trust him.

4. Full of hate; very intense. (ожесточённый, заклятый)

  1. The two brothers became bitter enemies.
  2. It was the bitterest election fight in years.

5. (Of weather) extremely cold. (лютый, пронизывающий)

  1. A bitter wind blew off the lake.
  2. Chicago had its bitterest winter in decades.

bittersweet#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɪtərˌswiːt/ · BIT-ur-sweet
Русский
горько-сладкий; радостный и грустный одновременно
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more bittersweet, most bittersweet
Contrast
In American recipes bittersweet chocolate is dark chocolate with only a little sugar.

Bittersweet has 2 distinct senses.

1. Happy and sad at the same time. (радостный и грустный одновременно)

  1. Graduation was a bittersweet day for her.
  2. The ending of the movie is bittersweet.

2. (Of chocolate) dark and only slightly sweet. (горький (о шоколаде))

  1. Melt six ounces of bittersweet chocolate.
  2. I prefer bittersweet chocolate to milk chocolate.

biweekly#

Pronunciation
/baɪˈwiːkli/ · by-WEEK-lee
Русский
раз в две недели; дважды в неделю
Type
classifying
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
bi- + weekly
Contrast
Ambiguous: in the US it usually means 'every two weeks' (a biweekly paycheck), but it can mean 'twice a week'. Every other week or twice a week is clearer. Also an adverb: We get paid biweekly.

Biweekly has 2 distinct senses.

1. Happening every two weeks. (проходящий раз в две недели)

  1. Most of us get a biweekly paycheck.
  2. The team has biweekly planning meetings.

2. Happening twice a week. (проходящий дважды в неделю)

  1. She has biweekly physical therapy, on Tuesdays and Fridays.
  2. The paper went from weekly to biweekly delivery, Mondays and Thursdays.

black-and-white#

Pronunciation
/ˌblæk ən ˈwaɪt/ · blak-uhn-WYT
Русский
чёрно-белый; однозначный
Type
color; also compound
Position
both — hyphenated before a noun; black and white after a verb
Comparison
not gradable — but in the second sense as, so, very are common: not as black and white as it looks
Contrast
In black and white (a noun phrase) means 'in writing': It's right there in black and white.

Black-and-white has 2 distinct senses.

1. Showing only black, white and gray, with no other colors. (чёрно-белый)

  1. Grandpa watched black-and-white TV as a kid.
  2. The old photos are all black and white.

2. Seeing things as simply right or wrong, with nothing in between. (однозначный, упрощённый (чёрно-белый))

  1. Life isn't as black and white as you think.
  2. He has a very black-and-white view of the world.

black-market#

classifying
Position

Comparison
not gradable
Form

Contrast
The noun is two separate words: goods sold on the black market. Only the adjective before a noun takes the hyphen.

Bought or sold illegally, outside the legal market.

  1. Agents seized thousands of black-market cigarettes.
  2. Black-market prices for the drug tripled last year.
  3. He paid a black-market dealer twice what the ticket was worth.

Black-tie means men wear a tuxedo; white-tie is even more formal. On invitations the dress code is written without a hyphen: Black tie optional.

Requiring formal evening clothes.

  1. They're going to a black-tie dinner at the museum.
  2. The gala is black-tie, so rent a tux.
  3. Is the wedding black-tie or casual?

blackened#

Pronunciation
/ˈblækənd/ · BLAK-uhnd
Русский
почерневший, закопчённый; (кулин.) обжаренный в специях
Type
participial
Position
both — the cooking sense only before a noun
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ed from blacken (black + -en)

Blackened has 2 distinct senses.

1. Made black by smoke, fire or dirt. (почерневший, закопчённый)

  1. Firefighters walked through the blackened remains of the house.
  2. The walls of the old kitchen were blackened with soot.
  3. He pulled a blackened pan out of the oven.

2. Of food, coated in spices and cooked quickly in a very hot pan until dark, in the Cajun style. (обжаренный в специях до тёмной корочки)

  1. I'll have the blackened catfish with rice.
  2. The restaurant is famous for its blackened chicken sandwich.

Not responsible for anything bad; having done nothing wrong.

bland#

Pronunciation
/blænd/ · BLAND
Русский
пресный, безвкусный; скучный, невыразительный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — blander, blandest

Bland has 2 distinct senses.

1. Of food, having very little flavor. (пресный, безвкусный)

  1. The soup was a little bland, so I added some salt.
  2. After the surgery he had to stick to a bland diet.
  3. This is the blandest salsa I've ever tasted.

2. Dull and uninteresting; without strong features or opinions. (скучный, невыразительный)

  1. The office was painted a bland beige.
  2. The senator gave a bland statement that said almost nothing.

blank#

Pronunciation
/blæŋk/ · BLANGK
Русский
пустой, чистый; безучастный, непонимающий
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Opposite
filled in; full
Contrast
The noun is common too: fill in the blanks, my mind drew a blank. A blank check is a signed check with no amount, and figuratively unlimited freedom.

Blank has 2 distinct senses.

1. With nothing written, printed or recorded on it. (пустой, чистый, незаполненный)

  1. Write your answer on a blank sheet of paper.
  2. Please don't leave any lines blank.
  3. The screen suddenly went blank.

2. Showing no expression, understanding or memory. (безучастный, непонимающий)

  1. When I asked about the money, he just gave me a blank look.
  2. As soon as the test started, my mind went blank.

blanket#

Pronunciation
/ˈblæŋkɪt/ · BLANG-kit
Русский
общий, огульный, всеобъемлющий
Type
classifying — covering every case
Position
attributive only — a blanket ban, never the ban was blanket
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
Much more often a noun (одеяло). As an adjective it only comes before a noun and means applying to everything, with no exceptions — often with disapproval: a blanket statement.

Applying to all cases or people, without exceptions.

  1. The city imposed a blanket ban on fireworks.
  2. That's a blanket statement, and it isn't fair to everyone.
  3. The policy offers blanket coverage for all employees.

blasphemous#

Pronunciation
/ˈblæsfəməs/ · BLAS-fuh-muhs
Русский

Type
evaluative
Position

Comparison

Form
from blasphemy
Contrast
The stress moves: the adjective and the noun are BLAS-fuh-, but the verb blaspheme is blas-FEEM.

Showing disrespect for God or for something religious; humorously, disrespecting something people treat as sacred.

  1. The film was banned in several countries as blasphemous.
  2. Some viewers found the painting blasphemous.
  3. In Chicago, putting ketchup on a hot dog is about the most blasphemous thing you can do.

blatant#

Pronunciation
/ˈbleɪtənt/ · BLAY-tuhnt
Русский
явный, вопиющий, неприкрытый
Type
evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more blatant, most blatant
Contrast
Blatant is about something bad done openly, without trying to hide it: a blatant lie. For something simply easy to see, use obvious.

Very obvious and done openly, without shame.

  1. That was a blatant lie, and everyone in the room knew it.
  2. The referee missed a blatant foul.
  3. It was the most blatant case of discrimination the lawyer had ever seen.

blazing#

Pronunciation
/ˈbleɪzɪŋ/ · BLAY-zing
Русский
пылающий, палящий; стремительный
Type
participial
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ing from blaze
Contrast
The fire was blazing is usually the verb blaze. Before another adjective, blazing works like an adverb: blazing hot, blazing fast.

Blazing has 2 distinct senses.

1. Burning strongly and brightly; of the sun or weather, extremely hot. (пылающий, палящий)

  1. We sat around a blazing campfire.
  2. The players were exhausted after two hours in the blazing sun.
  3. It was a blazing July afternoon in Phoenix.

2. Extremely fast. (стремительный, молниеносный)

  1. The new phone runs at blazing speed.
  2. He ran the forty-yard dash in a blazing 4.3 seconds.

Bleak has 2 distinct senses.

  1. The future looks bleak for the town's last factory.

2. Of a place or weather, cold, bare and unwelcoming. (унылый, голый, холодный)

  1. The prairie looks bleak in the middle of winter.

blended#

Pronunciation
/ˈblɛndɪd/ · BLEN-did
Русский

Type

Position

Comparison

Blended has 2 distinct senses.

1. Made by mixing different kinds or sources together. (смешанный, купажированный)

  1. This is a blended whiskey, not a single malt.
  2. The school uses blended learning, with some classes online.
  3. Pour in the blended fruit and stir.

2. Of a family, made up of a couple and the children from their earlier relationships. ((о семье) смешанная, с детьми от прежних браков)

  1. They're a blended family with five kids between them.
  2. Holidays can be complicated in blended families.

blessed#

Pronunciation
/blɛst; ˈblɛsɪd/ · BLEST; BLES-id
Русский
счастливый, одарённый; святой; благословенный
Type
quality; also classifying
Position
both — blessed with after a verb; a blessed relief before a noun
Comparison
more/most — more blessed, most blessed
Takes
with + noun
Contrast
The pronunciation follows the meaning. After a verb, lucky is one syllable: We're blessed (BLEST). Before a noun, holy and welcome are two: the Blessed Virgin, a blessed relief (BLES-id).

Blessed has 3 distinct senses.

1. Lucky to have something good; given a talent or advantage. Pronounced BLEST. (счастливый, одарённый)

  1. We've been blessed with three healthy kids.
  2. She's blessed with a great singing voice.
  3. I feel very blessed to have friends like you.

2. Holy; connected with God. Pronounced BLES-id. (святой, блаженный)

  1. The church is dedicated to the Blessed Virgin Mary.
  2. The pastor quoted the Bible: "It is more blessed to give than to receive."

3. Bringing welcome relief or happiness. Pronounced BLES-id. (благословенный, долгожданный)

  1. After a week of storms, the sunshine was a blessed relief.
  2. The kids finally fell asleep, and there was blessed silence.

Русский
костлявый, худой; костистый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — bonier, boniest
Form
bone + -y, with the e dropped

Bony has 2 distinct senses.

1. Very thin, so that the bones show. (костлявый, худой)

  1. The stray dog was bony and scared.
  2. He had long, bony fingers.
  3. She was the boniest kid in the class.

2. Of fish, full of small bones. (костистый, с множеством костей)

  1. Carp is tasty but very bony.
  2. I don't like bony fish.

Born-again has 2 distinct senses.

bothered#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɑːðərd/ · BAH-thurd
Русский
обеспокоенный, раздражённый
Type
participial; also emotion
Position
predicative only — I'm not bothered
Comparison
more/most — more bothered, most bothered
Takes
by or about + noun; that-clause; to-infinitive in can't be bothered to
Form
-ed from bother — how you feel
Contrast
Bothered is how you feel; bothersome is what causes it. Can't be bothered (to do something), meaning too lazy to make the effort, is more British; Americans often say I don't feel like it. Hot and bothered means upset or sexually excited.

Bothered has 2 distinct senses.

1. Worried, annoyed or upset by something. (обеспокоенный, раздражённый)

  1. I'm not bothered by the noise anymore.
  2. Were you bothered that he didn't call?
  3. She seemed more bothered about the car than about her injury.

2. Willing to make the effort; almost always in the negative. ((не) желающий утруждать себя)

  1. He couldn't be bothered to reply to my email.
  2. Some people just can't be bothered to recycle.

Comparison
not gradable
Opposite

Contrast
Much more common as a noun: at the bottom of the page. As an adjective it only comes before a noun. The bottom line is the final result or the most important fact.

Bottom has 2 distinct senses.

1. In the lowest position. (нижний)

  1. The towels are in the bottom drawer.
  2. Sign your name on the bottom line.
  3. The cereal is on the bottom shelf.

2. Lowest in rank, amount or quality. (последний, самый низкий)

  1. Students in the bottom half of the class got extra tutoring.
  2. Families in the bottom 20 percent pay almost no income tax.

bourgeois#

Pronunciation
/ˈbʊrʒwɑː/ · BUURZH-wah
Русский
буржуазный; мещанский
Type
evaluative; also classifying
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more bourgeois, most bourgeois
Form
from French bourgeois, a town dweller
Contrast
Also stressed at the end, boorzh-WAH. In everyday American speech it is usually a joke or a criticism: Avocado toast is so bourgeois. The noun for the class is the bourgeoisie.

Bourgeois has 2 distinct senses.

1. Belonging to or typical of the middle class, especially in political writing. (буржуазный)

  1. Marx wrote about the struggle against bourgeois society.
  2. She came from a comfortable bourgeois family.

2. Too concerned with comfort, money and respectability; conventional. (мещанский, обывательский)

  1. His artist friends think a house in the suburbs is hopelessly bourgeois.
  2. Their tastes got more bourgeois as they got richer.
  3. Is a twelve-dollar latte a little bourgeois?

Boxed in has 2 distinct senses.

1. Feeling trapped, with no freedom or no choices. (загнанный в угол, скованный)

  1. She felt boxed in by her parents' expectations.
  2. The governor is boxed in by the budget.

2. Physically surrounded so that you cannot move out. (зажатый, запертый со всех сторон)

  1. My car was boxed in by two delivery trucks.
  2. The runner got boxed in on the final lap.

not gradable

broad#

Pronunciation
/brɔːd/ · BRAWD
Русский
широкий; общий
Type
size; also quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — broader, broadest
Contrast
Broad and wide overlap for size (a broad/wide river), but broad is the word for shoulders, smile, terms, daylight and accent, and wide is the word for measurements: ten feet wide.

Broad has 4 distinct senses.

1. Wide from side to side. (широкий)

  1. He has broad shoulders.
  2. The river is broader here.
  3. She gave me a broad smile.

2. Including many different things or people. (широкий, разнообразный)

  1. The store sells a broad range of products.
  2. The plan has broad support in Congress.
  3. This is the broadest definition of the term.

3. General, without details. (общий, в общих чертах)

  1. In broad terms, the plan is simple.
  2. Can you give me a broad outline of the project?

4. Clear and obvious; of an accent, strong and noticeable. (явный, откровенный; сильный (об акценте))

  1. He was robbed in broad daylight.
  2. She dropped a broad hint that she wanted a ring.
  3. He speaks with a broad Texas accent.

broken#

Pronunciation
/ˈbroʊkən/ · BROH-kuhn
Русский
сломанный, разбитый; неработающий
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
past participle of break
Opposite
unbroken; working

Broken has 5 distinct senses.

1. Damaged, cracked or in pieces. (сломанный, разбитый)

  1. Watch out for the broken glass.
  2. She has a broken arm.
  3. The back window was broken.

2. Not working. (неисправный, неработающий)

  1. The elevator is broken again.
  2. We called a plumber about the broken dishwasher.

3. Of a promise, rule or agreement: not kept. (нарушенный)

  1. Voters are tired of broken promises.
  2. The broken treaty led to war.

4. Of a person, family or relationship: badly hurt or ruined. (сломленный; распавшийся)

  1. He grew up in a broken home.
  2. After losing his business, he was a broken man.
  3. She never got over her broken engagement.

5. Of a language: spoken badly and with many mistakes. (ломаный)

  1. He asked for directions in broken English.
  2. We got by with broken Spanish and hand signals.

/buːˈlɪmɪk/ · boo-LIM-ik
Русский
страдающий булимией; булимический
Type
classifying
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ic from bulimia

Having bulimia, an eating disorder in which a person eats a lot and then forces themselves to vomit.

  1. She was bulimic all through high school.
  2. The clinic treats bulimic and anorexic patients.
  3. He had no idea his roommate was bulimic.

bulky#

Pronunciation
/ˈbʌlki/ · BUHL-kee
Русский
громоздкий, объемный; грузный
Type
size
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — bulkier, bulkiest
Form
-y from bulk

Bulky has 2 distinct senses.

1. Large and hard to carry or store. (громоздкий, объемный)

  1. The old TVs were heavy and bulky.
  2. You can ship bulky furniture for an extra fee.
  3. The winter coat is bulkier than it looks online.

2. Of a person: big and heavy. (крупный, грузный)

  1. A bulky linebacker knocked him to the ground.
  2. He's gotten bulky from lifting weights.

bulletproof#

Pronunciation
/ˈbʊlɪtˌpruːf/ · BUUL-it-proof
Русский
пуленепробиваемый; неуязвимый
Type
compound — noun + adjective
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — bullet + -proof

Bulletproof has 2 distinct senses.

1. Made so that bullets cannot go through it. (пуленепробиваемый)

  1. The president's car has bulletproof glass.
  2. Officers wear bulletproof vests.
  3. The bank teller's window is bulletproof.

2. Informal: impossible to fail, defeat or criticize. (неуязвимый, безупречный)

  1. There's no such thing as a bulletproof business plan.
  2. The team looked bulletproof until the playoffs.

bullish#

Pronunciation
/ˈbʊlɪʃ/ · BUUL-ish
Русский

Type

Position
both
Comparison

Takes
on or about + noun
Form
-ish from bull, an investor who expects prices to rise
Opposite

Bullish has 2 distinct senses.

1. Of investors or markets: expecting prices to rise. (ожидающий роста, «бычий»)

  1. Investors are bullish on tech stocks.
  2. Analysts turned more bullish after the earnings report.

2. Confident and optimistic about the future. (оптимистичный, уверенный)

  1. The coach is bullish about the team's chances.
  2. She sounded bullish about the future of the company.

bumpy#

Pronunciation
/ˈbʌmpi/ · BUHM-pee
Русский
ухабистый; тряский; непростой
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — bumpier, bumpiest
Form
-y from bump
Opposite
smooth

Bumpy has 3 distinct senses.

1. Having an uneven surface. (ухабистый, неровный)

  1. The road to the cabin is bumpy.
  2. The rash made his skin red and bumpy.

2. Of a trip: shaking up and down. (тряский)

  1. It was a bumpy flight.
  2. The ride got bumpier as we climbed.

3. Full of problems or difficulties. (трудный, непростой)

  1. The company had a bumpy first year.
  2. The switch to the new system has been bumpy.

buoyant#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɔɪənt/ · BOY-uhnt
Русский
плавучий; жизнерадостный; устойчиво растущий
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison

Buoyant has 3 distinct senses.

1. Able to float. (плавучий)

  1. Salt water makes you more buoyant.
  2. Cork is light and buoyant.

2. Cheerful and confident. (жизнерадостный, бодрый)

  1. She was in a buoyant mood after the interview.
  2. The crowd was buoyant after the win.

3. Of prices, sales or the economy: rising or staying strong. (устойчивый, растущий)

  1. Sales stayed buoyant through the holidays.
  2. The housing market is still buoyant.

Burmese#

Position

Comparison

Form
-ese from Burma
Contrast
The country is officially Myanmar, and news writing often uses Myanmar as a modifier (the Myanmar military). Burmese is still the usual word for the language, the food and the cat breed.

Of or from Burma (Myanmar), its people or its language.

burned#

Pronunciation
/bɜːrnd/ · BURND
Русский
обгоревший, сгоревший, обожженный
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Form
past participle of burn
Contrast
Americans use burned for the past tense and usually for the adjective too; burnt is also common as an adjective, especially for food and colors (burnt toast, burnt orange).

Damaged, hurt or blackened by fire or heat.

  1. The burned building was torn down.
  2. His hand was badly burned.
  3. Throw out the burned cookies.

burned-out#

Pronunciation
/ˌbɜːrndˈaʊt/ · burnd-OWT
Русский
выгоревший; измотанный; перегоревший
Type
compound — participle + adverb
Position
both — hyphenated before a noun, usually burned out after a verb
Comparison
more/most — more burned out, most burned out
Form
compound — from the verb burn out

Burned-out has 3 distinct senses.

1. Exhausted and without energy or interest because of too much work or stress. (эмоционально выгоревший, измотанный)

  1. Many nurses feel burned out.
  2. Burned-out teachers are leaving the profession.
  3. I was more burned out after that job than I realized.

2. Destroyed by fire, with only the outside left. (выгоревший, сгоревший)

  1. We drove past burned-out cars.
  2. The burned-out factory has been empty for years.

3. Of a light bulb: no longer working. (перегоревший)

  1. Replace the burned-out bulb in the hallway.
  2. Two of the streetlights on our block are burned out.

burning#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɜːrnɪŋ/ · BUR-ning
Русский
горящий; жгучий; насущный
Type
participial
Position
both — attributive only in the sense 'strong, urgent'
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ing from burn

Burning has 3 distinct senses.

1. On fire. (горящий)

  1. Firefighters rushed into the burning building.
  2. We could smell burning rubber.

2. Very hot, or causing a hot, painful feeling. (обжигающий, жгучий)

  1. The sand was burning under our feet.
  2. I felt a burning pain in my chest.

3. Very strong or urgent. (страстный; насущный)

  1. She has a burning desire to succeed.
  2. The burning question is who will pay for it.

Contrast
Russian деловой is usually the noun business as a modifier: a business meeting, a business suit. Businesslike describes a manner: efficient, serious and not emotional.

Serious, practical and efficient.

button-down#

Pronunciation
/ˈbʌtnˌdaʊn/ · BUHT-uhn-down
Русский
с воротником на пуговицах; консервативный
Type
compound — noun + adverb
Position
attributive only — a button-down shirt
Comparison
not gradable — but very button-down in the sense 'conservative'
Form
compound — button + down
Contrast
A button-down (noun) is the shirt itself. In the sense 'conservative', button-down and buttoned-down mean the same.

Button-down has 2 distinct senses.

1. Of a shirt collar: fastened to the shirt with small buttons. (с воротником на пуговицах)

  1. He wore a blue button-down shirt and khakis.
  2. The school requires a white button-down shirt.
  3. Button-down collars look fine without a tie.

2. Conservative and conventional. (консервативный, строгий)

  1. It's a very button-down law firm.
  2. He left his button-down office job to become a chef.

buttoned-up#

Byzantine#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɪzənˌtiːn/ · BIZ-uhn-teen
Русский
византийский; запутанный
Type
origin; also quality
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable — but more byzantine in the sense 'complicated'
Contrast
In the sense 'too complicated' it is often written with a small b: byzantine tax rules.

Byzantine has 2 distinct senses.

1. Of the Byzantine Empire, its art or its church. (византийский)

  1. The museum has a room full of Byzantine mosaics.
  2. Constantinople was the capital of the Byzantine Empire.

2. Very complicated and hard to understand, often secretive. (запутанный, крайне сложный)

  1. The tax code is byzantine.
  2. Getting a visa involves Byzantine rules and endless paperwork.

Cagey has 2 distinct senses.

calculated#

Pronunciation
/ˈkælkjəˌleɪtɪd/ · KAL-kyuh-lay-tid
Русский
рассчитанный, преднамеренный
Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
more/most
Takes
to-infinitive (meant to have an effect)
Form
past participle of calculate
Contrast
Calculated describes a plan or act; calculating describes a person who plans selfishly: a calculated move by a calculating politician.

Calculated has 2 distinct senses.

1. Carefully and deliberately planned to have a particular effect. (рассчитанный, преднамеренный)

  1. The remark was a calculated insult.
  2. The ad is calculated to appeal to teenagers.
  3. Her decision to leave was cold and calculated.

2. Of a risk: taken after thinking carefully about what could happen. (обдуманный, взвешенный)

  1. Starting the business was a calculated risk.
  2. The coach took a calculated gamble in the fourth quarter.

calculating#

Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more calculating, most calculating

Contrast
Calculating is a person who plans selfishly; calculated is the plan or act.

Planning carefully to get what you want, often without caring about others.

  1. She's a cold, calculating businesswoman.
  2. Behind his friendly smile, he was calculating and ruthless.
  3. Her brother was even more calculating than she was.

Calibrated has 2 distinct senses.

  1. Make sure the scale is properly calibrated.
  2. The lab uses only calibrated thermometers.

2. Carefully planned or measured for a particular effect. (выверенный, взвешенный)

  1. The White House gave a carefully calibrated response.
  2. Her speech was finely calibrated to please both sides.

  1. One, two and three are cardinal numbers; first, second and third are ordinal.

After a verb it is an adverb phrase with no hyphens: We decide case by case.

Dealing with each situation separately instead of using one rule for all.

  1. Refunds are handled on a case-by-case basis.
  2. The school takes a case-by-case approach to late homework.
  3. The judge made case-by-case decisions about bail.

cast-iron#

Pronunciation
/ˈkæstˌaɪərn/ · KAST-y-urn
Русский

Type
material; also quality
Position

Comparison

Form

Cast-iron has 2 distinct senses.

1. Made of cast iron, a hard iron poured into a mold. (чугунный)

  1. My grandmother cooked everything in a cast-iron skillet.
  2. The park still has its old cast-iron benches.

2. Very strong or certain; impossible to break or doubt. (железный, несокрушимый)

  1. You need a cast-iron stomach to eat that chili.
  2. The suspect had a cast-iron alibi.

casual#

Pronunciation
/ˈkæʒuəl/ · KAZH-oo-uhl
Русский

Type
quality
Position

Comparison
more/most — more casual, most casual
Opposite
formal (of clothes and events); serious
Contrast
Russian случайный in the sense 'accidental' or 'random' is accidental, random or chance: a random choice, a chance meeting. Casual means relaxed, informal or not serious.

Casual has 3 distinct senses.

1. Relaxed, and not showing much worry or interest. (непринуждённый, небрежный, беспечный)

  1. He tried to sound casual, but he was nervous.
  2. She has a very casual attitude toward deadlines.

2. Of clothes, places or events: informal and suitable for everyday. (повседневный, неформальный)

  1. The dress code at our office is casual.
  2. It's a casual restaurant, so jeans are fine.
  3. Can I wear something more casual tonight?

3. Not regular, close or serious. (несерьёзный, поверхностный, нерегулярный)

  1. We're just casual friends.
  2. He's a casual fan who only watches the playoffs.
  3. Even a casual glance shows the numbers are wrong.

Caucasian#

Pronunciation
/kɔːˈkeɪʒən/ · kaw-KAY-zhuhn
Русский

Type
origin
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
A trap for Russian speakers: in American forms, police reports and medical records Caucasian means 'white', with no link to the Caucasus. A man from Dagestan or Georgia is not called Caucasian in everyday American speech; say from the Caucasus. Many Americans now simply say white.

Caucasian has 2 distinct senses.

1. White; of European ancestry, used as a formal racial label. (белый, европеоидный (о расе))

  1. The suspect was described as a Caucasian male in his thirties.
  2. The form asks whether you are Caucasian, Black, Asian or Hispanic.

2. Of or from the Caucasus region between the Black Sea and the Caspian Sea. (кавказский)

  1. The museum has a fine collection of Caucasian rugs.
  2. Chechen belongs to the family of Northeast Caucasian languages.

  1. I'm finally caught up on my email.
  2. Don't tell me what happens — I'm not caught up yet.

  1. The farm has to wait three years before its crops are certifiable as organic.
  2. Results must be accurate and certifiable.

cheap#

Pronunciation
/tʃiːp/ · CHEEP
Русский
дешёвый; некачественный; скупой; низкий
Type
quality; also evaluative
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — cheaper, cheapest
Opposite

Contrast
Cheap often suggests poor quality, so to praise a low price say inexpensive or affordable. About a person, American cheap means 'stingy' (жадный), not дешёвый. Cheaply is the adverb, though buy it cheap is common in speech.

Cheap has 4 distinct senses.

1. Low in price. (дешёвый, недорогой)

  1. Gas is cheaper in New Jersey than in New York.
  2. We found a cheap flight to Miami.

2. Of poor quality. (дешёвый, некачественный)

  1. The chair broke because it was made of cheap plastic.
  2. Her shoes looked cheap.

3. Of a person: not willing to spend money. (скупой, жадный)

  1. Don't be so cheap — leave a real tip.
  2. My uncle is too cheap to turn on the heat.

4. Unfair or unkind in a way that shows no respect. (низкий, подлый)

  1. Bringing up his divorce was a cheap shot.
  2. Jokes about her weight were a cheap way to get laughs.

chilling#

Pronunciation
/ˈtʃɪlɪŋ/ · CHIL-ing
Русский

Type
participial
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more chilling, most chilling
Form
-ing — what causes the feeling; from chill
Contrast
Chilling frightens you; chilled means made cold (chilled wine). In slang, We're just chilling is the verb chill, meaning relaxing, not this adjective.

Chilling has 2 distinct senses.

1. Frightening, especially in a cold, quiet way. (леденящий душу, жуткий)

  1. The movie tells a chilling story about a small-town murder.
  2. His calm description of the crime was chilling.
  3. The most chilling part was the silence afterward.

2. Making people afraid to act or speak freely; used mainly in a chilling effect. (сдерживающий, отпугивающий)

  1. Critics say the new law will have a chilling effect on free speech.
  2. The lawsuits had a chilling effect on reporters.

civic#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɪvɪk/ · SIV-ik
Русский
гражданский; городской
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
Civic is about a citizen's part in the life of a town or country (civic duty, civic pride). Civil is about citizens as opposed to the military or the church, and also means polite (civil rights, a civil answer). Civilian means not in the armed forces (civilian clothes).

Civic has 2 distinct senses.

1. Relating to the duties and activities of citizens. (гражданский)

  1. Voting is a civic duty.
  2. She's active in several civic organizations.
  3. The school teaches civic responsibility.

2. Relating to a town or city and its public life. (городской, муниципальный)

  1. The concert is at the civic center.
  2. The new park is a source of civic pride.

civil#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɪvəl/ · SIV-uhl
Русский
гражданский; вежливый
Type
classifying; also quality
Position
both — but civil meaning 'of citizens' or 'in law' goes only before a noun
Comparison
more/most — more civil, most civil in the 'polite' sense; the other senses are not gradable
Takes
to + person (polite sense)
Opposite
uncivil, rude (polite sense); military (citizens sense); criminal (law sense)
Contrast
Civil and civic both translate as гражданский: civil rights, civil war and a civil ceremony are about citizens as opposed to the army or the church, while civic duty is about taking part in public life. Civilian clothes (not civil clothes) are clothes that are not a uniform.

Civil has 3 distinct senses.

1. Relating to ordinary citizens and their rights, not to the military or religion. (гражданский)

  1. Martin Luther King Jr. was a leader of the civil rights movement.
  2. The country was torn apart by civil war.
  3. They had a civil ceremony at city hall instead of a church wedding.

2. In law: about private disputes between people or companies, not about crimes. (гражданский (о праве, иске))

  1. She filed a civil lawsuit against the company.
  2. It's a civil case, so nobody is going to prison.

3. Polite, but not especially friendly. (вежливый, корректный)

  1. Try to stay civil even if he gets angry.
  2. They're divorced, but they're civil to each other.
  3. The debate was more civil than anyone expected.

civilian#

Pronunciation
/sɪˈvɪljən/ · si-VIL-yuhn
Русский
гражданский, штатский
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only

Contrast
Usually a noun (Two civilians were hurt). The adjective goes before a noun: civilian clothes, civilian life; not civil clothes.

Not belonging to the armed forces or the police.

  1. He found it hard to adjust to civilian life after the Army.
  2. The bombing caused heavy civilian casualties.
  3. The officers were in civilian clothes.

Civilized has 2 distinct senses.

2. Polite, calm and reasonable; also pleasant and comfortable. (культурный, воспитанный; приличный)

  1. Let's discuss this like civilized adults.
  2. Dinner at eight is a much more civilized hour than dinner at five.
  3. The divorce was surprisingly civilized.

clean#

Pronunciation
/kliːn/ · KLEEN
Русский
чистый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — cleaner, cleanest
Opposite
dirty
Contrast
In I clean forgot and The bullet went clean through, clean is an informal adverb meaning 'completely'. Russian чистый in чистый вес or чистая прибыль is net in English: net weight, net profit, never clean.

Clean has 6 distinct senses.

1. Not dirty. (чистый, опрятный)

  1. Are these towels clean?
  2. Put on a clean shirt before dinner.
  3. The kitchen is cleaner than it's been in weeks.

2. Not causing or containing pollution. (экологически чистый, незагрязнённый)

  1. The state is investing in clean energy.
  2. Everyone has a right to clean drinking water.
  3. Electric buses are cleaner than diesel ones.

3. Free of crimes, drugs or anything wrong. (незапятнанный; (разг.) завязавший с наркотиками)

  1. He has a clean driving record.
  2. She's been clean for three years.
  3. The athlete's drug test came back clean.

4. Not offensive or sexual. (приличный, пристойный)

  1. Keep your jokes clean — there are kids here.
  2. It's a clean comedy the whole family can watch.

5. Smooth, simple and neat, with no rough or extra parts. (чёткий, ровный, аккуратный)

  1. I like the clean lines of modern furniture.
  2. It was a clean break, so the bone should heal quickly.
  3. The surgeon made a small, clean cut.

6. Fair and played by the rules. (честный, по правилам)

  1. It was a hard but clean game.
  2. Both candidates promised to run a clean campaign.

clinical#

Pronunciation
/ˈklɪnɪkəl/ · KLI-ni-kuhl
Русский
клинический; бесстрастный; стерильный
Type
classifying; also quality
Position
both — but the medical sense mostly before a noun
Comparison
more/most — more clinical in the 'cold' and 'bare' senses; the medical sense is not gradable
Contrast
About a person, a style or a room, clinical is usually criticism: cold and without feeling. It is not an intensifier like Russian клинический in клинический идиот.

Clinical has 3 distinct senses.

1. Relating to the examination and treatment of patients, not to theory or lab work. (клинический)

  1. The drug is still in clinical trials.
  2. She has twenty years of clinical experience as a nurse.
  3. He was diagnosed with clinical depression.

2. Cold and without emotion; efficient but unfeeling. (бесстрастный, холодный)

  1. She described the accident in a calm, clinical way.
  2. The team finished the game with clinical efficiency.
  3. His writing is a little too clinical for my taste.

3. Of a room or building: plain, very clean and unwelcoming, like a hospital. (стерильный, казённый)

  1. The new office feels cold and clinical.
  2. They painted over the clinical white walls with a warm yellow.

close#

Pronunciation
/kloʊs/ · KLOHS
Русский
близкий
Type
quality
Position
both — but close meaning careful or narrow usually goes before a noun: a close look, a close call
Comparison
-er/-est — closer, closest
Takes
to + noun or -ing
Preposition patterns
close to
Opposite
far, distant
Contrast
The adjective ends in an /s/ sound (KLOHS); the verb close, meaning shut, ends in /z/ (KLOHZ). Close is also an adverb (Come close; They live close by). For people, use close, not near: a close friend, never a near friend.

Close has 7 distinct senses.

1. Near in space or time. (близкий, недалёкий)

  1. The hotel is close to the beach.
  2. Christmas is getting close.
  3. Where's the closest gas station?

2. Knowing and liking each other very well. (близкий (о дружбе, отношениях))

  1. She's my closest friend.
  2. I'm very close to my brother.
  3. They have a close relationship.

3. Directly related in a family. (близкий (о родстве))

  1. Only close relatives were invited to the funeral.
  2. Her closest living relative is a cousin in Texas.

4. Won or lost by a very small difference. (равный, упорный (о борьбе))

  1. It was a close game until the last minute.
  2. The election is too close to call.
  3. This year's race was closer than last year's.

5. Careful and thorough. (пристальный, тщательный)

  1. Take a close look at the contract before you sign.
  2. Pay close attention to the instructions.

6. Very similar. (похожий, близкий)

  1. There's a close resemblance between the two sisters.
  2. The translation is very close to the original.

7. Almost happening, especially about something bad. (едва не случившийся (о беде))

  1. Wow, that was close — that truck almost hit us!
  2. We made our flight, but it was a close call.

closed#

Pronunciation
/kloʊzd/ · KLOHZD

Position
both
Comparison
not gradable — but more closed or very closed when it means not open to people or ideas

Contrast
A store that is not open is closed, not close: The bank is closed on Sundays. Close (KLOHS) is a different adjective meaning near.

Closed has 3 distinct senses.

1. Not open; shut, or not doing business. (закрытый)

  1. Keep the windows closed — it's freezing.

  1. The hearing was closed to the public.
  2. It's a very closed community.
  3. He has a closed mind when it comes to politics.

3. Finished and no longer being discussed or investigated. (закрытый, завершённый)

  1. As far as I'm concerned, the matter is closed.
  2. Police say the case is now closed.

  3. Seattle is much cloudier than Los Angeles.

2. Of a liquid or glass: not clear. (мутный)

  1. The water in the pool looks cloudy.
  2. Throw out the medicine if it turns cloudy.

cold#

Pronunciation
/koʊld/ · KOHLD
Русский
холодный; замёрзший; неприветливый
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
-er/-est — colder, coldest
Opposite
hot; warm
Contrast
To say you feel cold, English uses the person as subject: I'm cold = мне холодно, not «я холодный». It's cold is about the weather or a room. A cold is a noun (простуда): I have a cold.

Cold has 4 distinct senses.

1. Having a low temperature. (холодный)

  1. It's cold outside, so take a jacket.
  2. January is usually the coldest month in Chicago.
  3. Can I get a cold glass of water?

2. Feeling that your body is not warm enough. (замёрзший (мне холодно))

  1. I'm cold — can you close the window?
  2. The kids were wet and cold after the game.

3. Unfriendly and showing no warm feelings. (холодный, неприветливый)

  1. She gave me a cold look and walked away.
  2. His voice got colder when I mentioned the money.

4. Unconscious, especially after being hit; used in out cold. (без сознания)

  1. The boxer was out cold for a full minute.
  2. He hit his head on the ice and was knocked out cold.

cold-blooded#

Pronunciation
/ˌkoʊld ˈblʌdɪd/ · kohld-BLUH-did
Русский

Type

Position

Comparison
more/most — more cold-blooded, most cold-blooded
Form
compound — adjective + noun + -ed
Opposite

Contrast
Russian хладнокровный is usually praise (calm under pressure); English cold-blooded is always negative about people — it means cruel. For calm, say cool-headed or calm.

Cold-blooded has 2 distinct senses.

1. Cruel and showing no emotion. (жестокий, безжалостный)

  1. It was a cold-blooded murder.
  2. The jury saw him as a cold-blooded killer.
  3. That was the most cold-blooded thing I've ever seen.

2. Having a body temperature that changes with the surroundings, like reptiles and fish. (холоднокровный)

  1. Snakes are cold-blooded, so they lie in the sun.
  2. Cold-blooded animals slow down in cold weather.

Comparison
more/most — more colorful, most colorful
Form
-ful from color
Opposite

Colorful has 3 distinct senses.

1. Having many bright colors. (красочный, яркий)

  1. The market was full of colorful fruit.
  2. Her kitchen is much more colorful than mine.

2. Interesting, lively and a little unusual. (колоритный, яркий)

  1. My grandfather was a colorful character.
  2. The town has a long and colorful history.

3. Of language, rude or full of swearing; a polite way to say it. ((о языке) крепкий, грубый)

  1. The coach used some colorful language after the loss.
  2. His description of his boss was colorful, to say the least.

comfortable#

Pronunciation

Say it in three syllables: KUHMF-tur-buhl, not com-FOR-ta-ble. The person is the subject for how you feel: I'm comfortable = мне удобно; The chair is comfortable = стул удобный.

Comfortable has 4 distinct senses.

  1. They live a comfortable life in the suburbs.

4. Of a lead or win, large enough to be safe. (уверенный (о победе, отрыве))

  1. The Yankees had a comfortable lead in the ninth.
  2. She won by a comfortable margin.

common#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑːmən/ · KAH-muhn
Русский
распространённый, частый; общий; обычный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison

Opposite

Contrast
Russian общий is wider: the total cost (not common cost), general knowledge (not common knowledge, which means "something everybody knows"). Common sense is a noun — здравый смысл.

Common has 3 distinct senses.

1. Happening often or existing in large numbers. (распространённый, частый)

  1. Smith is the most common last name in the US.
  2. It's common for kids to be afraid of the dark.

2. Shared by two or more people or groups. (общий)

  1. We have a common interest in jazz.
  2. This problem is common to all big cities.

3. Ordinary; not special or of high rank. (обычный, простой)

  1. There's no cure for the common cold.
  2. He talks like a common working man.

common-law#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑːmən ˌlɔː/ · KAH-muhn-law
Русский
гражданский (о браке без регистрации); основанный на общем праве
Type
compound; also classifying
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
compound — common + law
Contrast
A false friend: Russian гражданский брак in everyday speech means living together without registering — that is common-law marriage. English civil marriage is a registered marriage without a religious ceremony.

Common-law has 2 distinct senses.

1. Of a husband, wife or marriage, treated as married because the couple has lived together for a long time. (гражданский, незарегистрированный (о браке))

  1. She's been his common-law wife for ten years.
  2. Only a few states still recognize common-law marriage.

2. Based on law made by past court decisions rather than written statutes. (основанный на общем (прецедентном) праве)

  1. The lawyer made a common-law argument.
  2. Fraud is also a common-law offense.

2. Able to live or work together happily. (подходящий друг другу, совместимый)

  1. We're just not compatible as roommates.
  2. The app matches you with the most compatible people nearby.

3. Able to exist together without conflict. (совместимый, согласующийся)

  1. Is economic growth compatible with protecting the environment?
  2. His actions aren't compatible with his promises.

compelling#

Pronunciation

Type

Compelling has 2 distinct senses.

  2. Give me one compelling reason to stay.

2. So interesting that you want to keep watching, reading or listening. (захватывающий)

competent#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑːmpɪtənt/ · KAHM-puh-tuhnt
Русский
компетентный, умелый; вполне приличный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more competent, most competent
Takes
at or in + noun or -ing; to-infinitive (legal: competent to stand trial)
Opposite

Contrast
English competent often means only "good enough": a competent performance is solid, not brilliant. Russian компетентный sounds like higher praise — for that, say highly skilled or expert.

Competent has 3 distinct senses.

1. Having enough skill or knowledge to do something well. (компетентный, умелый)

  1. We need a competent lawyer.
  2. She's very competent at managing people.
  3. He's more competent than his résumé suggests.

2. Satisfactory but not excellent. (вполне приличный, добротный)

  1. The band gave a competent but boring show.
  2. His Spanish is competent, not fluent.

3. Legally able to make decisions or take part in a trial. (дееспособный, правомочный)

  1. The judge ruled that he was competent to stand trial.
  2. A doctor must confirm the patient is mentally competent.

Confirmed has 2 distinct senses.

Confounding has 2 distinct senses.

  1. The disappearance remains one of the most confounding mysteries of the decade.
  2. The new tax rules are confounding even to accountants.

2. In research, affecting the result in a way that hides the real cause. (искажающий, вмешивающийся (о факторе))

  1. Age was a confounding factor in the study.
  2. The researchers tried to control for confounding variables.

Conscious has 3 distinct senses.

  1. We made a conscious decision not to have a TV.
  2. It takes a conscious effort to slow down.

Conservative has 3 distinct senses.

/ˈkɑːnsələr/ · KAHN-suh-lur
Русский
консульский
Type
classifying
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ar from consul

Relating to a consul or a consulate.

  1. The embassy offers consular services to citizens abroad.
  2. He asked to speak to a consular officer.
  3. You'll need to schedule a consular interview for your visa.

consultative#

Type
classifying
Position
both — mostly before a noun
Comparison
not gradable
Form
-ive from consult

Giving advice rather than making decisions.

  1. The committee has a purely consultative role.
  2. She takes a consultative approach with her clients.
  3. The council's function is consultative, not legislative.

consumable#

Form

Contrast
The plural noun consumables means supplies that get used up, such as ink or paper.

Meant to be used up and then replaced, or able to be eaten or used.

  1. The budget covers consumable supplies like paper and ink.
  2. Filters are consumable parts that need replacing.
  3. Only consumable items were allowed in the care package.

consummate#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑːnsəmət/ · KAHN-suh-muht
Русский
непревзойдённый, совершенный
Type
evaluative; also emphasizing
Position
attributive only
Comparison
not gradable
Contrast
The verb consummate ends in /eɪt/: KAHN-suh-mayt. The adjective ends in a weak /ət/.

Extremely skilled; perfect in a particular role.

  1. She's a consummate professional.
  2. He played the piece with consummate skill.
  3. The senator was a consummate politician.

contagious#

Pronunciation
/kənˈteɪdʒəs/ · kuhn-TAY-juhs
Русский
заразный; заразительный
Type
quality
Position
both
Comparison
more/most — more contagious, most contagious

Contagious has 2 distinct senses.

1. Of a disease or person, able to pass illness to others by contact. (заразный, инфекционный)

  1. Measles is one of the most contagious diseases.
  2. Stay home while you're still contagious.

2. Of a feeling or behavior, quickly spreading to other people. (заразительный)

  1. She has a contagious laugh.
  2. His enthusiasm was contagious.

contemporary#

Pronunciation
/kənˈtɛmpəˌrɛri/ · kuhn-TEM-puh-rer-ee
Русский
современный
Type
age; also classifying
Position
both
Comparison
not gradable — but more contemporary of style and design
Takes
with + noun — in the 'same period' sense
Contrast
Contemporary can mean 'of today' or 'of that time': contemporary accounts of the Civil War were written during the war, not recently.

Contemporary has 2 distinct senses.

1. Belonging to the present time; modern. (современный)

  1. The museum focuses on contemporary art.
  2. The house has a clean, contemporary design.
  3. Her music is very contemporary.

2. Living or happening at the same time as a particular person or event in the past. (современный той эпохе, того времени)

  1. Contemporary newspapers described the fire in detail.
  2. Shakespeare was contemporary with Cervantes.

Contrast
Contemptible describes what deserves contempt; contemptuous describes the person who feels it. His lie was contemptible; she was contemptuous of him.

So bad or dishonest that it deserves no respect.

  1. Stealing from a charity is contemptible.
  2. It was the most contemptible thing he'd ever done.
  3. She called the ad a contemptible lie.

Showing that you think someone or something is worthless.

Contrast
Content means the same but normally comes only after a verb (I'm content); contented also goes before a noun (a contented sigh).

Happy and satisfied with what you have.

  1. The baby gave a contented sigh and fell asleep.
  2. They seem contented with their quiet life.
  3. I've never seen him more contented.

contentious#

Pronunciation

Position

Contentious has 2 distinct senses.

1. Likely to cause disagreement. (спорный, вызывающий разногласия)

  1. Immigration remains a contentious issue.
  2. The new stadium plan is highly contentious.

2. Full of arguments, or tending to argue. (ожесточённый, конфликтный)

  1. They went through a long, contentious divorce.
  2. It was the most contentious board meeting in years.

The contiguous United States means the 48 states that border each other, without Alaska and Hawaii.

Touching or next to each other, with no gap.
